(Appendix
C.3
), we consider a clever decomposition of the suboptimality gap and carefully handles the error coming from an estimated robust set size.
The resulting suboptimality bound does not explode as
\rho_{0}\rightarrow 0
and is nearly-minimax optimal on the dependence of key parameters (Section
5.2.2
).

6
Numerical Experiments

The goals of experiments.

We demonstrate the sample-efficiency of the proposed two algorithms and the robustness of the learned assortment through simulated numerical experiments.
To this end, we aim to study three key perspectives of this problem, namely:

(i)

The sample efficiency of the proposed algorithms v.s. naive baselines (introduced later). See Section
6.1
.

(ii)

The robustness of the learned assortments and the comparison of the robustness properties under the two different formulations, i.e., Example
2.1
and Example
2.2
. See Section
6.2
.

(iii)

The influence of the cardinality constraint
K
on the sample complexity. See Section
6.3
.

We conduct three lines of experiments to investigate these perspectives respectively in the following.

6.1
Experiment 1: Sample Efficiency of Pessimistic Robust Rank Breaking.

In the first line of experiments, we demonstrate the sample efficiency of P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B(-C/V) compared with naive baselines in terms of minimizing the suboptimality of the robust expected revenue of the learned assortments.
Here the baseline is implemented by the
single-pessimism
counterpart of P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B(-C/V).
More concretely, for Example
2.1
, we consider the following learned assortment,

\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{\mathrm{vanilla}}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{S\subset[N],|S|\leq K}R_{\rho}(S;\widehat{\bm{v}}),\quad\widehat{v}_{j}:=\frac{\widehat{p}_{j}}{1-\widehat{p}_{j}},\quad\text{$\widehat{p}_{j}$ defined in \eqref{eq: tau}}.

(6.1)

and for Example
2.2
, we consider

\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{\mathrm{vanilla}}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{S\subset[N],|S|\leq K}\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\,\widetilde{H}(S,\lambda;\widehat{\bm{v}}),\quad\widehat{v}_{j}:=\frac{\widehat{p}_{j}}{1-\widehat{p}_{j}},\quad\text{$\widehat{p}_{j}$ defined in \eqref{eq: tau} and $\widetilde{H}$ defined in \eqref{eq: h tilde}}.

(6.2)

They represent the methods non-pessimistic to the data uncertainty which simply plug in the non-pessimistic rank-breaking estimate
\widehat{\bm{v}}
of the attraction parameters to the robust expected revenue.

Figure 3:
Suboptimality gap of P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-C (Algorithm
1
) compared to the single-pessimistic counterpart (
6.1
). All the parameter configurations are averaged over
25
independent runs.

Figure 4:
Suboptimality gap of P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-V (Algorithm
2
) compared to single-pessimism counterpart (
6.2
). All the parameter configurations are averaged over
25
independent runs.

Construction of the instances and data.

We construct the following instance of nominal choice model (as motivated by the hard instances in the proof of statistical lower bounds, see Appendices
B.4
and
C.4
).
We consider
N=15
and
K=3
.
We define the nominal choice model by

\displaystyle v_{j}=\frac{1}{K}+\epsilon,\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq K,\quad\text{and}\quad v_{i}=\frac{1}{K},\quad\forall K+1\leq i\leq N.

(6.3)

with
\epsilon=0.01
and define
r_{i}=1
for any
1\leq i\leq N
.
To show case the efficiency of our proposed algorithms compared to the vanilla algorithms we just introduced, we construct a partial coverage style offline dataset satisfying the “robust item-wise coverage” condition.
Specifically, we solve the optimal robust assortment (which is unique under the above setup) and randomly substitute an element in it by an item not inside the optimal robust assortment.
Such a dataset has two key features: (i) the optimal robust assortment as a whole is not observed across the entire dataset; (ii) the suboptimal items are observed far less frequent than the items in the optimal robust assortment, causing essentially larger uncertainty for those items compared with the items in the optimal robust assortment, making the dataset a partially covered one.

Experiment results.

We vary two key quantities of the problem and observe the suboptimality gap of the learned assortment for both Example
2.1
and Example
2.2
.
For both examples, we range the dataset size from
12000
to
180000
.
For Example
2.1
, we vary
\rho\in\{0.05,0.1,0.15,0.2,0.25,0.3,0.35,0.4,0.45,0.5\}
, and for Example
2.2
, we vary
\rho_{0}\in\{0.05,0.075,0.1,0.125,0.15,0.175\}
.
All the experiment parameters are run for
25
times and we take the average.
See Figures
3
and
4
.
We observe that our Pessimistic Rank-breaking algorithms consistently outperform the vanilla algorithms in terms of the sample efficiency across different examples, sample sizes, and robust set parameters.
Especially, given the same sample size, our algorithms achieve much smaller suboptimality gap than the vanilla baseline, demonstrating their efficiency.

Moreover, as we can see from the experiments varying the robust set size parameters (right figures), the suboptimality gap shrinks when the robust set size parameter grows, matching the theoretical predictions (see Theorem
B.1
and Theorem
C.1
, which predict a decaying suboptimality if increasing the robust set size).

6.2
Experiment 2: Robustness of the Learned Assortment.

In the second line of experiments, we aim to demonstrate the robustness of the learned assortment by the robust assortment optimization framework we consider.
Our approach is by showing how well our robust data-driven algorithms can bring revenue improvement against state-of-the-art non-robust counterpart
(
Han et al. 2025
)
when the customer preference distribution shifts from data generating patterns.

Experiment pipeline.

We first describe the pipeline of this experiment.
We fix a nominal MNL model as the data generation environment, denoted by
\bm{v}_{0}
.
We also fix a general non-uniform revenue.
From the nominal environment
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{v}_{0}}
, we generate choice data
i_{k}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\bm{v}_{0}}(\cdot|S_{k})
based on randomly sampled assortments
S_{k}
, forming a dataset of size
n=20000
.
Then we conduct P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-C (Algorithm
1
), P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-V (Algorithm
2
), and PRB
(
Han et al. 2025
)
.
In specific, we range the robust set parameters for P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B(-C/V) to obtain a series of robust assortments.
We note that the non-robust algorithm PRB can be obtained from P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B by setting the robust set parameter to be zero.

Then to test the robustness properties of the learned assortments, we deploy the series of assortments in perturbed choice distributions.
We consider the following way of perturbing the choice distribution.
We treat
\bm{p}_{0}:=\bm{v}_{0}/\sum_{j\in[N]_{+}}v_{0,j}
as a prior distribution over the whole items and we perturb such a prior distribution to obtain different choice models with different prior distributions.
In our experiments, we randomly generate the perturbed prior distributions
\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}
, denoted as the set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{perturb}}
.
For each of the
\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{perturb}}
, we calculate the suboptimality gap under the MNL model
\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{0}
corresponding to
\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}
between the learned assortments and the counterfactual optimal assortment
S^{\star}_{\text{non-robust}}(\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{0})
.
After ranging over the randomly generated perturbation set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{perturb}}
, we look into the statistics (defined later) of how the assortments given by our proposed robust assortment learning algorithms could improve the expected revenue over the non-robust assortment under shifted choice distributions.

We take
N=50
,
K=50
.
For P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-C, the robust set size is taken over
\rho\in\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{c}}:=\{0,0.1,0.2,\cdots,0.9,1.0\}
, and for P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-V, the robust set parameter
\rho_{0}
is taken over
\rho_{0}\in\mathscr{R}_{\mathrm{v}}:=\{0,0.02,0.04,\cdots,0.18,0.2\}
.
Finally, to investigate the influence of the degree of the perturbation on the robustness properties, we divide the set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{perturb}}
into
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{perturb}}^{[0,1)}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{perturb}}^{[1,\infty)}
.
Here
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{perturb}}^{[0,1)}
means that any
\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{perturb}}^{[0,1)}
satisfies
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}\|\bm{p}_{0})<1
, and similarly for the other set.
We let each of the two sets contain
10000
randomly perturbed prior distributions.

Figure 5:
Results for experiments on robustness of the learned assortment. The first column is the distribution for the absolute improvement
\Delta
. The second column is the distribution of the relative improvement
\widetilde{\Delta}
, and the last column is the distribution of the optimal robust parameter. The first two rows are for P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-C, and the last two rows are for P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-V.

Evaluation metrics and results.

Properly evaluating the robustness of a learned model is a non-trivial task.
To test the robustness of the assortments learned by our algorithms, we look into three statistics.

The first statistics is the distribution of the improvement brought by using robust assortment learning approaches.
More specifically, we define the random variable
\Delta
with instantiation on
\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}
as

\displaystyle\Delta(\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}):=\sup_{\rho\in\mathscr{R}}R(\widehat{S}_{\rho};\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{0})-R(\widehat{S}_{0};\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{0}),\quad\forall\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{perturb}},

(6.4)

and we plot the histogram of the distribution of
\Delta
.
This visualizes the distribution of largest possible revenue the robust approach could bring compared to non-robust method.
For completeness, we consider the second statistics to be the distribution of another variable
\widetilde{\Delta}
, defined as the relative improvement,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}(\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}):=\frac{\Delta(\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0})}{R(\widehat{S}_{0};\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{0})}.

(6.5)

The third statistics is defined as another random variable
\varrho
with instantiation on
\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}
as

\displaystyle\varrho(\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}):=\mathop{\mathrm{argsup}}_{\rho\in\mathscr{R}}R(\widehat{S}_{\rho};\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{0})-R(\widehat{S}_{0};\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{0}),\quad\forall\widetilde{\bm{p}}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{perturb}}.

(6.6)

This informs us under random choice pattern shifts what is the distribution of an optimal robust parameter.
By looking into the distribution of
\varrho
under P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-C and P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-V, we also gain insights into the difference between the formulation of Example
2.1
and Example
2.2
.

The results are shown in Figure
5
. In general, the robust assortments is able to bring significant revenue improvement compared to non-robust assortment.
Under extreme circumstances, the robust assortment can bring up to 25% revenue improvement.
For larger choice distribution shifts, the advantage of the robust assortment over the non-robust assortment is much more significant.
This can be observed by comparing the first (third) row to the second (fourth) row.
In the meanwhile, for larger choice distribution shifts, one can observe that the optimal choice of the robust set size parameter also becomes larger, meaning that the larger distributional shift necessitates a larger robust set size.

6.3
Experiment 3: Influence of Cardinality Constraints.

In the last line of experiments, we aim to demonstrate the theoretical predictions regarding the influence of the cardinality constraints on the suboptimality of the learned assortments.
As predicted by the theory, given the same effective sample size
\min_{i\in S^{\star}}n_{i}
, the learned robust assortment suffer larger suboptimality if the robust assortment optimization problem involves a larger cardinality constraint
K
, see Theorem
5.1
and
5.3
.
Also, for both Examples
2.1
and
2.2
, the uniform revenue case suffers a lower order dependence of
K
, i.e.,
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(K^{1/2})
, compared to the general non-uniform revenue case of
\mathcal{O}(K)
.
We aim to verify this by experiments.

Construction of the instances and data.

We construct the following instances of the nominal choice model and the observational data following the same protocol as in the proof of statistical lower bounds, see Appendices
B.4
and
C.4
.
We consider
N=50
,
K\in\{1,2,\cdots,9,10\}
, and
n_{\mathrm{effect}}:=\min_{i\in S^{\star}}n_{i}=1000
.
For the uniform revenue case and each
K\in\{1,2,\cdots,9,10\}
, we define the nominal choice model by

\displaystyle v_{j}=\frac{1}{K}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{effect}}\cdot K}},\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq K,\quad\text{and}\quad v_{i}=\frac{1}{K},\quad\forall K+1\leq i\leq N,

(6.7)

and in this case the revenues are uniform, i.e.,
r_{i}=1
for any
1\leq i\leq N
.
For the general non-uniform revenue case and each
K\in\{1,2,\cdots,9,10\}
, we define the nominal choice model by

\displaystyle v_{j}=\frac{1}{K}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{effect}}}},\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq K;\quad v_{i}=\frac{1}{K},\quad\forall K+1\leq i\leq 4K;\quad v_{k}=1,\quad\forall 4K+1\leq k\leq N.

(6.8)

Then the non-uniform revenues are defined as

\displaystyle r_{j}=1,\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq 4K;\quad r_{i}=0,\quad\forall 4K+1\leq i\leq N.

(6.9)

Finally, the offline dataset is defined as following.
We let
n_{\mathrm{total}}=4Kn_{\mathrm{effect}}
and for each
1\leq k\leq n_{\mathrm{total}}
,

\displaystyle S_{k}:=\left\{\left\lceil\frac{k}{n_{\mathrm{effect}}}\right\rceil,4K+2,\cdots,5K\right\}.

(6.10)

Experiment results.

We plot the suboptimality gap of the robust expected revenue of P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B(-C/V) with respect to cardinality sizes
K
separately for uniform and non-uniform revenue cases.
See Figures
6
and
7
.
As we can see from the relation between the logarithm of suboptimality gap and the logarithm of size constraints, it matches our theoretical predictions of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(K^{1/2})
dependency for the special case of uniform revenue and
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(K)
dependency for the general case of non-uniform revenue.
The only slight exception is for the varying robust set size case (Example
2.2
, Figure
7
) where the dependency deviates a bit from prediction when the size constraint
K=1
.
Beyond
K=1
, the result well matches our theory.

Finally, we also vary the robust set parameters and plot the suboptimality gap to cardinality size
K
for different parameters.
Specifically, for Example
2.1
, we consider
\rho\in\{0.1,0.15,0.2,0.25,0.3,0.35,0.4,0.45,0.5\}
, and for Example
2.2
, we consider
\rho_{0}\in\{0.005,0.01,0.015,0.02,0.025\}
.
The results are provided in Figures
8
and
9
.
It further confirms our theory that for both two examples the uniform revenue setup enjoys a lower order of suboptimality gap than the non-uniform revenue setup for different robust set parameters.

Figure 6:
Suboptimality gap of P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-C (Algorithm
1
) with respect to the cardinality constraint. All the parameter configurations are averaged over
25
independent runs.

Figure 7:
Suboptimality gap of P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-V (Algorithm
2
) with respect to the cardinality constraint. All the parameter configurations are averaged over
25
independent runs.

Figure 8:
Suboptimality gap of P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-C (Algorithm
1
) with respect to the cardinality constraint for different robust set size
\rho
. All the parameter configurations are averaged over
25
independent runs.

Figure 9:
Suboptimality gap of P
\mathrm{R}^{2}
B-V (Algorithm
2
) with respect to the cardinality constraint for different robust set parameter
\rho_{0}
. All the parameter configurations are averaged over
25
independent runs.

7
Conclusions

In this work, we addressed the limitations of traditional data-driven assortment optimization methods, which often falter under shifting customer preferences and model misspecification. To mitigate these challenges, we introduced a robust framework that accounts for distributional uncertainty in customer choice behavior, aiming to safeguard against worst-case revenue outcomes. We demonstrated that robust assortment planning remains computationally tractable even when incorporating uncertainty, and we extended this framework to the data-driven setting by developing statistically optimal algorithms that balance robustness with sample efficiency. Our theoretical contributions include tight bounds on sample complexity and the introduction of “robust item-wise coverage” as a key concept for enabling efficient learning. These results underscore the importance of designing algorithms that generalize reliably under uncertainty, offering both practical tools and theoretical insights for data-driven robust assortment optimization.

Appendix A
Algorithms and Proofs for Solving the Optimal Robust Assortment with Known Nominal Model

A.1
Proof of Proposition
3.1

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Proposition
3.1
hold for Example
2.1
due to Theorem 2 of
Jin et al. 2022a
.
We now prove this conclusion for Example
2.2
.
By the dual representation (Proposition
2.1
), any assortment
S\subseteq[N]
is optimal if and only if there exists
\lambda\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}}\right)

(A.1)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\sup_{S^{\prime}\subseteq[N],\lambda^{\prime}\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda^{\prime}\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda^{\prime})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}_{+}}v_{i}-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\right\}:=R^{\star},

(A.2)

which is further equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}\cdot\Big(\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)-\exp(-R^{\star}/\lambda)\Big)\leq\exp(-R^{\star}/\lambda)-1-\exp(-R^{\star}/\lambda)\cdot(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}.

(A.3)

Consider the complete set assortment
S^{\star}:=\{1,\cdots,i^{\star}\}
with
r_{i}\geq R^{\star}
for all
i\in S^{\star}
.
We can find that such
S^{\star}
is a minimizer of the left hand side of (
A.3
) regardless of specific
\lambda\geq 0
.
Therefore, such assortment
S^{\star}
must be an optimal robust assortment, because otherwise, for any assortment
S
and any dual
\lambda\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}\cdot\Big(\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)-\exp(-R^{\star}/\lambda)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}v_{i}\cdot\Big(\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)-\exp(-R^{\star}/\lambda)\Big)

(A.4)

\displaystyle>\exp(-R^{\star}/\lambda)-1-\exp(-R^{\star}/\lambda)\cdot(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i},

(A.5)

which contradicts the feasibility of the optimality condition.
This proves Proposition
2.1
.
∎

A.2
Proof of Proposition
3.2

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

We first prove the conclusion for Example
2.1
.
By the dual representation (Proposition
2.1
), for any assortment
S\subseteq[N]
with
|S|\leq K
,

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S;\bm{v})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}}\right)-\lambda\cdot\rho\right\}

(A.6)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)+\frac{1-\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}}\right)-\lambda\cdot\rho\right\}.

(A.7)

Thus to maximize the robust expected revenue, it suffices to find
S
such that
\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}
is maximized, which is equivalent to find the assortment with top
K
attraction parameter
v_{i}
. This proves for Example
2.1
.

Now we prove the conclusion for Example
2.2
.
Similarly, by the dual representation, for any assortment
S\subseteq[N]
with
|S|\leq K
, we have that

\displaystyle R_{\rho(\cdot;\cdot)}(S;\bm{v})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\right\}

(A.8)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)+\frac{1-\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i})}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\right\}.

(A.9)

under the condition that
\rho_{0}\leq\log(1+1/v_{\mathrm{tot}})
, we know that
1-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i})>0
.
Thus the robust expected revenue is maximized when
\sum_{i\in S}
is maximized, which is equivalent to find the assortment with top
K
attraction parameter
v_{i}
. This proves for Example
2.1
and finishing the proof of Proposition
3.2
.
∎

A.3
Proof of Proposition
3.3

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

The algorithm and analysis for the general case under Example
2.1
is presented by
Jin et al. 2022a
in Section 3.3.
We refer the readers to their work for the details.
In the coming subsections, we give the algorithm design and analysis for Example
2.2
.
These combined prove Proposition
3.3
.
∎

A.3.1
Algorithm Design for Varying Robust Set Size (Example
2.2
)

Our algorithm design is based upon the geometric perspective for assortment optimization introduced in
Rusmevichientong et al. 2010
.
We first introduce the basic observations for the problem and present the intuitions of the algorithm design, after which we present the full algorithms and the theoretical guarantees.

Intuitions.

Recall that here the goal is to (approximately) solve the following optimization problem,

\displaystyle S^{\star}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{S\subseteq[N],|S|\leq K}R_{\rho(\cdot;\cdot)}(S;\bm{v}),\quad R^{\star}:=\max_{S\subseteq[N],|S|\leq K}R_{\rho(\cdot;\cdot)}(S;\bm{v})=R_{\rho(\cdot;\cdot)}(S^{\star};\bm{v})

(A.10)

with the robust set size function
\rho(\cdot;\cdot)
given in Example
2.2
, the nominal parameter
\bm{v}
known, and a given constraint size
K
.
By the dual representation (Proposition
2.1
), it suffices to equivalently solve the following,

\displaystyle(S^{\star},\lambda^{\star}):=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[N],|S|\leq K\\
0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})\end{subarray}}H_{\rho(\cdot;\cdot)}(S,\lambda;\bm{v}),

(A.11)

where the function
H
is defined as

\displaystyle H_{\rho(\cdot;\cdot)}(S,\lambda;\bm{v})

\displaystyle:=-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}}\right)-\lambda\cdot\rho_{0}+\lambda\cdot\log\left(e^{\rho_{0}}-\frac{(e^{\rho_{0}}-1)\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}}\right)

(A.12)

\displaystyle=-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}}\right),

(A.13)

and the upper bound of the dual variable
\lambda
is defined as

\displaystyle B(S,\bm{v}):=\frac{r_{\max}}{\log\left(\frac{\sum_{j\in S_{+}}v_{j}}{\sum_{j\in S_{+}}v_{j}-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{j\in[N]_{+}}v_{j}}\right)}.

(A.14)

To design an efficient computational algorithm for the above optimization problem, it is useful to introduce an auxiliary variable
t
as following,

\displaystyle R^{\star}

\displaystyle=\max_{S\subseteq[N],|S|\leq K,0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\right\}

(A.15)

\displaystyle=\max_{0\leq t\leq r_{\max}}\left\{\exists S\subseteq[N],|S|\leq K,\lambda\in[0,B(S,\bm{v})],-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\geq t\right\}

(A.16)

\displaystyle=\max_{0\leq t\leq r_{\max}}\left\{\exists S\subseteq[N],|S|\leq K,\lambda\in[0,B(S,\bm{v})],\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}\left(\exp\big((t-r_{i})/\lambda\big)-1\right)\leq-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\!\!\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}\right\}

(A.17)

\displaystyle=\max_{0\leq t\leq r_{\max}}\left\{\min_{S\subseteq[N],|S|\leq K,0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})}\Bigg\{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}\left(\exp\big((t-r_{i})/\lambda\big)-1\right)\Bigg\}\leq-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}\right\}.

(A.18)

For notational simplicity, we define the following function,

\displaystyle G(t):=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[N],|S|\leq K\\
0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t,\lambda),\quad g_{i}(t,\lambda):=v_{i}\cdot\left(\exp\big((t-r_{i})/\lambda\big)-1\right),

(A.19)

under which we can reformulate the original optimization problem (
A.10
) via

\displaystyle R^{\star}=\sup_{0\leq t\leq r_{\max}}\left\{G(t)\leq-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}\right\}.

(A.20)

Remark A.1
.

Notice that if we find the
\epsilon
-optimal solution
t_{\epsilon}
of (
A.20
) and assortment
S(t_{\epsilon})
associated with
t_{\epsilon}
in the sense of the following,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\min_{0\leq\lambda\leq B(S(t_{\epsilon}),\bm{v})}\sum_{i\in S(t_{\epsilon})_{+}}g_{i}(t_{\epsilon},\lambda)\leq-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i},\quad\lambda^{\star}(t_{\epsilon}):=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{0\leq\lambda\leq B(S(t_{\epsilon}),\bm{v})}\sum_{i\in S(t_{\epsilon})_{+}}g_{i}(t_{\epsilon},\lambda),

(A.21)

then
S(t_{\epsilon})
is an
\epsilon
-optimal robust assortment.
This is because according to (
A.21
), it holds that

\displaystyle\lambda^{\star}(t_{\epsilon})\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S(t_{\epsilon})_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda^{\star}(t_{\epsilon}))}{\sum_{i\in S(t_{\epsilon})_{+}}v_{i}-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\geq t_{\epsilon}\geq R^{\star}-\epsilon,

(A.22)

where the first inequality is by (
A.21
), and the second inequality is by the
\epsilon
-optimality of
t_{\epsilon}
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle R_{\rho(\cdot;\cdot)}(S(t_{\epsilon});\bm{v})

\displaystyle=\max_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S(t_{\epsilon})_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S(t_{\epsilon})_{+}}v_{i}-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\right\}

(A.23)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda^{\star}(t_{\epsilon})\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S(t_{\epsilon})_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda^{\star}(t_{\epsilon}))}{\sum_{i\in S(t_{\epsilon})_{+}}v_{i}-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot\sum_{i\in[N]_{+}}v_{i}}\right)

(A.24)

\displaystyle\geq R^{\star}-\epsilon.

(A.25)

Therefore,
S(t_{\epsilon})
is an
\epsilon
-optimal robust assortment.

In the following, we focus on how to solve the reformulated problem (
A.20
).
Obviously we can see that
G(t)
is monotonically increasing with respect to
t
, and thus (
A.20
) can be efficiently solved by a bisection search if we can evaluate
G(t)
efficiently for any fixed
t
.

Now notice that
G
can be equivalently formulated as

\displaystyle G(t):=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\mathcal{N}(t),|S|\leq K\\
0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t,\lambda),\quad\mathcal{N}(t):=\big\{i\in[N]:r_{i}\geq t\big\}.

(A.26)

where we restrict the assortment inside the set
\mathcal{N}(t)
whose items have revenues larger than
t
.
Otherwise including an item with revenue smaller than
t
would not help minimize the summation.
If
|\mathcal{N}(t)|\leq K
, then
S(t)=\mathcal{N}(t)
.
Else, if
|\mathcal{N}(t)|>K
, we then need to decide the
K
items
i
with the smallest values
g_{i}(t,\lambda)
given any
\lambda
.
That is, we need to efficiently determine the order of the
|\mathcal{N}(t)|
curves
\{g_{i}(t,\lambda)\}_{\lambda\geq 0,i\in\mathcal{N}(t)}
.

To this end, we have the following lemma, which shows that the intersection points between these curves are well controlled.
This is beneficial because between the intersection points the order of the curves are fixed and thus evaluating
G(t)
reduces to optimizing over
\lambda
between these intersection points.
Moreover, it shows that the summation of
g_{i}(t,\lambda)
forms a quasi-convex function over
\lambda
, and thus the optimization over
\lambda
can be performed efficiently via bisection search.

Lemma A.2
.

Fix
t\geq 0
, the number of intersection points among curves
\{g_{i}(t,\lambda)\}_{\lambda\geq 0,i\in[N]}
is less than
N(N-1)/2
. Furthermore, for any assortment set
S\subset[N]
, it holds that
\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t,\lambda)
is a quasi-convex function in
\lambda
.

Proof of Lemma
A.2
.

We begin with proving the first claim on the number of intersection points.
It suffices to prove that any two curves
g_{i}(t,\lambda)
and
g_{j}(t,\lambda)
intersect at no more than one point.
Consider any different
i,j\in[N]
with
(v_{i},r_{i})\neq(v_{j},r_{j})
.
By symmetry, without loss of generality we assume that
v_{i}\geq v_{j}
.
We prove the claim by discussing three different cases.

•

Case 1:

v_{i}>v_{j}
and
r_{i}\geq r_{j}
. In this case, we have that for any
\lambda\geq 0
,

\displaystyle g_{i}(t,\lambda)=v_{i}\cdot\left(\exp\big((t-r_{i})/\lambda\big)-1\right)<v_{j}\cdot\left(\exp\big((t-r_{j})/\lambda\big)-1\right)=g_{j}(t,\lambda).

(A.27)

Therefore, we directly conclude that there is no intersection points between these two curves.

•

Case 2:

v_{i}>v_{j}
and
r_{i}<r_{j}
. In this case, consider that the existence of intersection points is equivalent to the existence of
\lambda
such that

\displaystyle\phi_{i,j}(\lambda):=v_{i}\cdot\exp\big((t-r_{i})/\lambda\big)-v_{j}\cdot\exp\big((t-r_{j})/\lambda\big)=v_{i}-v_{j}>0.

(A.28)

Notice that
\phi_{i,j}(0)=0
and that the derivative of
\phi_{i,j}
is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\phi_{i,j}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\cdot\Big(v_{i}(r_{i}-t)\cdot\exp\big((t-r_{i})/\lambda\big)-v_{j}(r_{j}-t)\cdot\exp\big((t-r_{j})/\lambda\big)\Big).

(A.29)

We can figure out the behavior of
\phi_{i,j}
by considering the following sub-cases:

–

Case 2.1:

v_{i}(r_{i}-t)<v_{j}(r_{j}-t)
.
In this case, the derivative
\partial_{\lambda}\phi_{i,j}(\lambda)
has a unique zero point
\lambda_{0}=(r_{j}-r_{i})/(\log(v_{j}(r_{j}-t))-\log(v_{i}(r_{i}-t)))
, and
\partial_{\lambda}\phi_{i,j}(\lambda)\geq 0
on
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{0}]
and
\partial_{\lambda}\phi_{i,j}(\lambda)\leq 0
on
[\lambda_{0},\infty)
.
Thus
\phi_{i,j}(\lambda)
is increasing on
[0,\lambda_{0}]
and is decreasing on
[\lambda_{0},\infty)
.
Noticing that the limit
\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow+\infty}\phi_{i,j}(\lambda)=v_{i}-v_{j}
and
\phi_{i,j}(0)=0
, we can conclude that there is a single
\lambda_{i,j}\in[0,\lambda_{0}]
such that
\phi_{i,j}(\lambda_{i,j})=v_{i}-v_{j}
.
Therefore, there is only one intersection point.

–

Case 2.2:

v_{i}(r_{i}-t)\geq v_{j}(r_{j}-t)
.
In this case, the derivative
\partial_{\lambda}\phi_{i,j}(\lambda)
is always non-negative and thus
\phi_{i,j}(\lambda)
is increasing.
Due to the limit
\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow+\infty}\phi_{i,j}(\lambda)=v_{i}-v_{j}
, we can see that there is no
\lambda
such that
\phi_{i,j}(\lambda)=v_{i}-v_{j}
.
Consequently, there is no intersection point for this case.

•

Case 3:

v_{i}=v_{j}
. In this case, no matter
r_{i}>r_{j}
or
r_{i}<r_{j}
, it is obvious to find that
g_{i}(t,\lambda)
and
g_{j}(t,\lambda)
can only intersect at
\lambda=0
, which means that there is only one intersection point.

Therefore, we have proved the first claim of Lemma
A.2
.

Now we prove the second claim of Lemma
A.2
. For any
S\subset[N]
, let
g_{S}(t,\lambda):=\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t,\lambda)
.
We have

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}g_{S}(t,\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\cdot\left(-v_{0}t\cdot\exp\big(t/\lambda\big)+\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}(r_{i}-t)\cdot\exp\big((t-r_{i})/\lambda\big)\right):=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\cdot m(t,\lambda)

(A.30)

To investigate the sign of
\partial_{\lambda}g_{S}(t,\lambda)
, we consider the derivative of
m(t,\lambda)
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}m(t,\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\cdot v_{0}t^{2}\cdot\exp\big(t/\lambda\big)+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\cdot\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}\cdot(r_{i}-t)^{2}\cdot\exp\big((t-r_{i})/\lambda\big)>0,

(A.31)

which means that
m(t,\lambda)
is increasing over
\lambda
.
Now that
\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}m(t,\lambda)=-\infty
, we conclude that
\partial_{\lambda}g_{S}(t,\lambda)
is either always negative or is negative on
[0,\lambda_{1}]
for some
\lambda_{1}>0
and then positive on
[\lambda_{1},\infty)
.
This means that
g_{S}(t,\lambda)
is either monotonically decreasing on
\lambda
or decreasing on
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{1}]
and increasing on
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\infty)
.
In both cases
g_{S}(t,\lambda)
is a quasi-convex function.
This completes the proof of Lemma
A.2
.
∎

Now with Lemma
A.2
and all the above discussions, we are ready to give the computational algorithm to solve the original optimization problem (
A.10
) and (
A.20
).

Algorithm to solve the optimal robust assortment for Example
2.2
.

The algorithm is presented in the following Algorithms
3
and Algorithm
4
.

Algorithm 3

Solving the optimal robust assortment (Example
2.2
)

1:

Inputs:
Error level
\epsilon,\epsilon_{G}\geq 0
, target
G
-value
a^{\dagger}(\rho_{0},\bm{v})
.

2:

Initialize
t_{\mathrm{left}}=0
and
t_{\mathrm{right}}=r_{\max}
.

3:

// Use bisection search to solve the largest possible
t
.

4:

for
iteration
m=1,\cdots,M_{\epsilon}:=\lceil\log(4r_{\max}/\epsilon)/\log 2\rceil

do

5:

Set
t_{\mathrm{mid}}=(t_{\mathrm{left}}+t_{\mathrm{right}})/2
and call Algorithm
4
to approximately solve
G(t_{\mathrm{mid}})
up to error
\epsilon_{G}
, obtain the returned value
\widehat{G}
and the assortment
S
.

6:

if

\widehat{G}>a^{\dagger}(\rho_{0},\bm{v})+\epsilon_{G}

then

7:

Set the right point
t_{\mathrm{right}}=t_{\mathrm{mid}}
.

8:

else

if

\widehat{G}<a^{\dagger}(\rho_{0},\bm{v})

then

9:

Set the left point
t_{\mathrm{left}}=t_{\mathrm{mid}}
.

10:

else

11:

break

12:

end

if

13:

end

for

14:

Set
t^{\prime}=t_{\mathrm{mid}}-\epsilon/2
and call Algorithm
4
to approximately solve
G(t^{\prime})
up to error
\epsilon_{G}
, and obtain the corresponding assortment
S
.

15:

Output:
value
t^{\prime}
and assortment
S

Algorithm 4

Approximately evaluate the function
G

1:

Inputs:
Error level
\epsilon_{G}\geq 0
, real number
t
, functions
\{g_{i}(t,\lambda)\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}(t),\lambda\geq 0}
, the intersection points among the functions
\{g_{i}(t,\lambda)\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}(t),\lambda\geq 0}
, denoted in an ascent order as
0=\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{I(t)}<\lambda_{I(t)+1}:=\infty
.

2:

// Case 1:

|\mathcal{N}(t)|\leq K
, directly set the assortment
S=\mathcal{N}(t)
.

3:

if

|\mathcal{N}(t)|\leq K

then

4:

Set the assortment
S=\mathcal{N}(t)

5:

Approximately solve the following minimization problem up to error
\epsilon_{G}
via bisection search:

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})}\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t,\lambda),

(A.32)

and denote the
\epsilon_{G}
-optimal value as
\widehat{G}
.

6:

Output:
assortment
S
and approximate value
\widehat{G}
.

7:

end

if

8:

// Case 2:

|\mathcal{N}(t)|>K
, need to enumerate over possible optimal assortments.

9:

for
interval
\ell=1,\cdots,I(t),I(t)+1

do

10:

Obtain
S_{\ell}
containing the
K
functions in
\{g_{i}(t,\lambda)\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}(t),\lambda_{\ell-1}\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_{\ell}}
with the
K
smallest values.

11:

if

B(S_{\ell},\bm{v})<\lambda_{\ell-1}

then

12:

continue

13:

end

if

14:

Approximately solve the following minimization problem up to error
\epsilon_{G}
via bisection search:

\displaystyle\min_{\lambda_{\ell-1}\leq\lambda\leq B(S_{\ell},\bm{v})\wedge\lambda_{\ell}}\sum_{i\in(S_{\ell})_{+}}g_{i}(t,\lambda),

(A.33)

and denote the
\epsilon_{G}
-optimal value as
\widehat{G}_{\ell}
.

15:

end

for

16:

Set
\widehat{\ell}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\ell\in[I(t)+1]}\widehat{G}_{\ell}
, and set
\widehat{G}=\widehat{G}_{\widehat{\ell}}
and
S=S_{\widehat{\ell}}
.

17:

Output:
assortment
S
and approximate value
\widehat{G}
.

Remark A.3
.

About solving the intersection points, it is a simple one-dimensional numerical equation, for which assume that we have solved all of them accurately before running Algorithms
3
and
4
. Actually, for any two
i,j\in[N]
, the cases such that the intersection point exists reduce to either a trivial case
\lambda_{\mathrm{intersect}}=0
or to solving the zero point of a monotone function (see Case 3 and Case 2.1 in Lemma
A.2
respectively).

Remark A.4
.

In the data driven algorithm Algorithm
2
, we need to solve a slightly different objective than (
A.10
).
More concretely, we assume the knowledge of the true value of
\sum_{i\in[N]}v_{i}
, denoted as
v_{\mathrm{tot}}
, and need to solve the new duality objective (
4.8
). However, the resulting new objective shares the same form as the objective
(
4
)
here except that the target
G
-value becomes
-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot(1+v_{\mathrm{tot}})
.
Therefore, it suffices to define the quantity
a^{\dagger}(\rho_{0},\bm{v}):=-(1-e^{-\rho_{0}})\cdot(1+v_{\mathrm{tot}})
in the input of Algorithm
3
.

A.3.2
Analysis of Algorithm Design for Varying Robust Set Size (Example
2.2
)

Theorem A.5
.

Given an error level
\epsilon<R^{\star}/2
, by setting the inner loop error level
\epsilon_{G}
as

\displaystyle\epsilon_{G}=\frac{v_{0}\epsilon}{4B(\emptyset,\bm{v})},

(A.34)

then the following two conclusions hold:

1.

Algorithm
3
returns a value
R^{\star}\geq t^{\prime}\geq R^{\star}-\epsilon
and an assortment
S
satisfying condition (
A.21
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})}\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t^{\prime},\lambda)\leq a^{\dagger}(\rho_{0},\bm{v}).

(A.35)

2.

The iteration complexity of Algorithm
3
is of order
\mathcal{O}(\log(r_{\max}/\epsilon))
, and in each iteration of Algorithm
3
, the sub-algorithm Algorithm
4
needs to evaluate functions in the form of
\sum_{i\in S}g_{i}(t,\cdot)
and its first-order derivative for no more than
\mathcal{O}(N^{2}\cdot\log(B(S,\bm{v})/\epsilon_{G}^{\prime}))
times, where
\epsilon_{G}^{\prime}
is defined as the largest positive real number such that

\displaystyle\epsilon_{G}^{\prime}\cdot\sup_{\lambda\in[0,B(S,\bm{v})]\cap[\lambda_{t}^{\star}(S,\bm{v})-\epsilon_{G}^{\prime},\lambda_{t}^{\star}(S,\bm{v})+\epsilon_{G}^{\prime}]}\left|\sum_{i\in S_{+}}\partial_{\lambda}g_{i}(t,\lambda)\right|\leq\epsilon_{G},

(A.36)

where
\lambda_{t}^{\star}(S,\bm{v}):=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})}\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t,\lambda)
for any tuple
(t,S,\bm{v})
.
Overall, the running complexity of Algorithm
4
is of order
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(N^{2})
.

Proof of Theorem
A.5
.

We first prove the second result regarding the running complexity of Algorithms
3
and
4
.
The first conclusion regarding the iteration complexity of Algorithm
3
is trivial given that Algorithm
3
iterates for no more than
M_{\epsilon}=\lceil\log(4r_{\max}/\epsilon)/\log 2\rceil
times.
Regarding the second conclusion for the running complexity of each call of Algorithm
4
, we need to invoke Lemma
A.2
that proves the intersection between the curves
\{g_{i}(t,\lambda)\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}(t),\lambda\geq 0}
for any
t
is no more than
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
.
More specifically, in Algorithm
4
, if
Case 1
holds (see Line
2
), then we only need evaluate the function
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}(t)_{+}}g_{i}(t,\cdot)
for
\mathcal{O}(\log(B(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v})/\epsilon_{G}^{\prime}))
times to use bisection search to solve the minimization problem (
A.32
) up to error level of
\epsilon_{G}^{\prime}
in terms of
\lambda
.
This is because within these many calls of the quasi-convex function
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}(t)_{+}}g_{i}(t,\cdot)
and its derivative, bisection search returns
\widehat{\lambda}_{t}(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v})
satisfying
|\widehat{\lambda}_{t}(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v})-\lambda^{\star}_{t}(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v})|\leq\epsilon_{G}^{\prime}
, and thus

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}(t)_{+}}g_{i}(t,\widehat{\lambda}_{t}(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v}))-\min_{0\leq\lambda\leq B(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v})}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}(t)_{+}}g_{i}(t,\lambda)

(A.37)

\displaystyle\qquad=\left|\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}(t)_{+}}g_{i}(t,\widehat{\lambda}_{t}(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v}))-\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}(t)_{+}}g_{i}(t,\lambda^{\star}_{t}(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v}))\right|

(A.38)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{\lambda^{\star}_{t}(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v})\wedge\widehat{\lambda}_{t}(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v})}^{\lambda^{\star}_{t}(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v})\vee\widehat{\lambda}_{t}(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v})}\left|\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}(t)_{+}}\partial_{\lambda}g_{i}(t,\lambda)\right|\mathrm{d}\lambda

(A.39)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\epsilon_{G}^{\prime}\cdot\sup_{\lambda\in[0,B(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v})]\cap[\lambda_{t}^{\star}(S,\bm{v})-\epsilon_{G}^{\prime},\lambda_{t}^{\star}(S,\bm{v})+\epsilon_{G}^{\prime}]}\left|\sum_{i\in S_{+}}\partial_{\lambda}g_{i}(t,\lambda)\right|

(A.40)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\epsilon_{G},

(A.41)

where the last inequality uses the condition on the parameter
\epsilon_{G}^{\prime}
in (
A.36
).
Thus
\widehat{\lambda}_{t}(\mathcal{N}(t),\bm{v})
is an
\epsilon_{G}
-optimal solution to (
A.10
).
Then, if
Case 2
holds (see Line
8
), by Lemma
A.2
, we know that the number of the intervals
|I(t)|=\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
, and thus we only need to solve the optimization problem in the form of (
A.33
) for less than
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
times.
Using arguments similar to those for
Case 1
, we prove that for each instance of the optimization problem (
A.33
), we need to evaluate the function
\sum_{i\in S_{\ell}}g_{i}(t,\cdot)
and its derivative for no more than
\mathcal{O}(\log(B(S_{\ell},\bm{v})/\epsilon_{G}^{\prime})
times to obtain an
\epsilon_{G}
-optimal solution.
Finally, we note that the process to find the
K
functions with the
K
smallest values in the
\ell
-th interval (Line
10
) only requires
\mathcal{O}(N)
times of evaluation of these functions due to the fact that there are no intersection points among these functions inside each of the interval.
This proves the second result of Theorem
A.5
.

Now we prove the first result of Theorem
A.5
regarding the correctness of Algorithm
3
.
Firstly, by the above proofs for the second result, we see that given value
t
queried by Algorithm
3
, Algorithm
4
returns an approximated value
\widehat{G}
of
G(t)
within error level
\widehat{G}\leq G(t)+\epsilon_{G}
, together with an assortment
S
satisfying
G(t)\leq\min_{0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})}\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t,\lambda)\leq\widehat{G}
.
With such a fact, the bisection process of Algorithm
3
can always maintain the optimal
t
value, i.e.,
t^{\star}:=R^{\star}
, inside the remaining interval
[t_{\mathrm{left}},t_{\mathrm{right}}]
.
This is because:

•

If
\widehat{G}>a^{\dagger}(\rho_{0},\bm{v})+\epsilon_{G}
, then
G(t^{\star})<\widehat{G}-\epsilon_{G}\leq G(t_{\mathrm{mid}})
.
Thus
t^{\star}\leq t_{\mathrm{mid}}
due to monotonicity of
G
.

•

Else if
\widehat{G}<a^{\dagger}(\rho_{0},\bm{v})
, then
G(t_{\mathrm{mid}})\leq\widehat{G}<G(t^{\star})
. Thus
t_{\mathrm{mid}}\leq t^{\star}
due to monotonicity of
G
.

•

Else if
a^{\dagger}(\rho_{0},\bm{v})\leq\widehat{G}\leq a^{\dagger}+\epsilon_{G}
, we are going to show that it must hold that
t^{\star}-\epsilon/4\leq t_{\mathrm{mid}}\leq t^{\star}+\epsilon/4
.
It suffices to prove
G(t^{\star}-\epsilon/4)\leq G(t_{\mathrm{mid}})\leq G(t^{\star}+\epsilon/4)
.
We first show the left side by proving the following inequality:
G(t^{\star}-\epsilon/4)+\epsilon_{G}\leq G(t^{\star})
, with which we can obtain that
G(t^{\star}-\epsilon/4)\leq G(t^{\star})-\epsilon_{G}\leq\widehat{G}-\epsilon_{G}\leq G(t_{\mathrm{mid}})
.
To prove that
G(t^{\star}-\epsilon/4)+\epsilon_{G}\leq G(t^{\star})
, consider the following,

\displaystyle G(t^{\star})-G(t^{\star}-\epsilon/4)

\displaystyle=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\mathcal{N}(t),|S|\leq K\\
0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t^{\star},\lambda)-\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\mathcal{N}(t),|S|\leq K\\
0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t^{\star}-\epsilon/4,\lambda)

(A.42)

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\mathcal{N}(t),|S|\leq K\\
0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t^{\star},\lambda)-g_{i}(t^{\star}-\epsilon/4,\lambda)\right\}

(A.43)

\displaystyle=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\mathcal{N}(t),|S|\leq K\\
0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum_{i\in S_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\Big(\exp\big((t^{\star}-r_{i})/\lambda\big)-\exp\big((t^{\star}-r_{i}-\epsilon/4)/\lambda\big)\Big)\right\}

(A.44)

\displaystyle\geq\min_{0\leq\lambda\leq B(\emptyset,\bm{v})}v_{0}\cdot\Big(\exp\big(t^{\star}/\lambda\big)-\exp\big((t^{\star}-\epsilon/4)/\lambda\big)\Big)

(A.45)

\displaystyle=\min_{0\leq\lambda\leq B(\emptyset,\bm{v})}v_{0}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{4\lambda}\cdot\exp\big(\zeta(t^{\star},t^{\star}-\epsilon/4)/\lambda\big)

(A.46)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{v_{0}\epsilon}{4B(\emptyset,\bm{v})}

(A.47)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{G},

(A.48)

where the first inequality is due to
\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)-\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g(x)\geq\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\{f(x)-g(x)\}
, the second inequality is by the fact that each summand is positive and the objective is minimized when
S=\emptyset
, the second to last equality is by mean-value theorem with
\zeta(t^{\star},t^{\star}-\epsilon/4)\in[t^{\star}-\epsilon/4,t^{\star}]
,
and the last equality is by the choice of
\epsilon_{G}
.
This proves that
G(t^{\star}-\epsilon/4)+\epsilon_{G}\leq G(t^{\star})
and therefore
G(t^{\star}-\epsilon/4)\leq G(t_{\mathrm{mid}})
.
By the same argument, we can also prove that
G(t^{\star})+\epsilon_{G}\leq G(t^{\star}+\epsilon/4)
, for which we can further obtain that
G(t_{\mathrm{mid}})\leq\widehat{G}\leq G(t^{\star})+\epsilon_{G}\leq G(t^{\star}+\epsilon/4)
.
This proves that if
a^{\dagger}(\rho_{0},\bm{v})\leq\widehat{G}\leq a^{\dagger}+\epsilon_{G}
, it must hold that
t^{\star}-\epsilon/4\leq t_{\mathrm{mid}}\leq t^{\star}+\epsilon/4
, and we can terminate the iteration now.

Therefore, when the iterations of Algorithm
3
end, we must have that
t^{\star}-\epsilon/4\leq t_{\mathrm{mid}}\leq t^{\star}+\epsilon/4
.
Then we have that
t^{\prime}=t_{\mathrm{mid}}-\epsilon/2
satisfying
t^{\star}-\epsilon<t^{\star}-3\epsilon/4\leq t^{\prime}\leq t^{\star}-\epsilon/4<t^{\star}
.
Furthermore, the corresponding assortment
S
satisfies

\displaystyle\min_{0\leq\lambda\leq B(S,\bm{v})}\sum_{i\in S_{+}}g_{i}(t^{\prime},\lambda)\leq G(t^{\prime})+\epsilon_{G}\leq G(t^{\star}-\epsilon/4)+\epsilon_{G}\leq G(t^{\star})=a^{\dagger}(\rho_{0},\bm{v}).

(A.49)

This completes the proof of Theorem
A.5
.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for Constant Robust Set Size Case

B.1
General Upper Bound for Suboptimality Gap

Theorem B.1

(Suboptimality of Algorithm
1
)
.

Conditioning on the observed assortments
\{S_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
, suppose that

\displaystyle n_{i}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{i\in S_{k}\}\geq 138\cdot\max\left\{1,\frac{256}{138v_{i}}\right\}\cdot(1+v_{\max})(1+Kv_{\max})\log(3N/\delta),\quad\forall i\in S^{\star},

(B.1)

then with probability at least
1-2\delta
, the robust expected revenue of the assortment
\widehat{S}
satisfies

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(\widehat{S};\bm{v})

(B.2)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{\mathsf{C}(\rho)\cdot r_{\max}}{1+v(S^{\star})}\cdot\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\left(16\sqrt{\frac{v_{i}(1+v_{i})\log(3N/\delta)}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{i_{k}\in S_{k}\}\cdot(1+v(S_{k}))^{-1}}}+\frac{88(1+v_{i})\log(3N/\delta)}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{i_{k}\in S_{k}\}\cdot(1+v(S_{k}))^{-1}}\right).

(B.3)

where
v(S):=\sum_{j\in S}v_{j}
and the function
\mathsf{C}(\rho)
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}(\rho)=\min\left\{\inf_{c\in[0,1/\rho]}\Big\{c\cdot\max\big\{2,\exp(1/c)-1\big\}\Big\},\frac{1}{\rho}\right\}.

(B.4)

Proof of Theorem
B.1
.

Please refer to Appendix
B.2
for a detailed proof of Theorem
B.1
.
∎

B.2
Proof of Theorem
B.1

Proof of Theorem
B.1
.

Consider the following upper bound of the suboptimality of the learned assortment,

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(\widehat{S};\bm{v})\leq R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(\widehat{S};\bm{v}^{\mathrm{LCB}})\leq R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v}^{\mathrm{LCB}}),

(B.5)

where the first inequality uses Lemma
D.1
which shows that the robust expected revenue is non-decreasing with respect to the parameter given the optimal robust assortment, and the second inequality is due to the optimality of
\widehat{S}
.
Now we can further bound the right-hand side of the above inequality by the following,

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v}^{\mathrm{LCB}})

\displaystyle=\sup_{0\leq\lambda\leq r_{\max}/\rho}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}\right)-\lambda\cdot\delta\right\}

(B.6)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sup_{0\leq\lambda\leq r_{\max}/\rho}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}\right)-\lambda\cdot\delta\right\},

(B.7)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0\leq\lambda\leq r_{\max}/\rho}\left\{\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}\right)\right\}.

(B.8)

Now using the inequality that
\log(a/b)=\log(1+(a-b)/b)\leq(a-b)/b
for all
a,b>0
, we can further bound the above expression by

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v}^{\mathrm{LCB}})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0\leq\lambda\leq r_{\max}/\rho}\left\{\lambda\cdot\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}-1\right)\right\}.

(B.9)

To derive a tight upper bound on the right hand side of the above inequality, we consider two ranges of the dual variable
\lambda
separately.
More specifically, we consider

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v}^{\mathrm{LCB}})

(B.10)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\max\left\{\underbrace{\sup_{0\leq\lambda\leq c\cdot r_{\max}}\left\{\lambda\cdot\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}-1\right)\right\}}_{\displaystyle{\text{Term (i)}}},\right.

(B.11)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.\underbrace{\sup_{c\cdot r_{\max}\leq\lambda\leq r_{\max}/\rho}\left\{\lambda\cdot\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}-1\right)\right\}}_{\displaystyle{\text{Term (ii)}}}\right\},

(B.12)

where
c>0
is a parameter to be tuned later.
We now upper bound
Term (i)
and
Term (ii)
separately.

Upper bounding
Term (i).

To upper bound
Term (i)
, we utilize the inequality that

\displaystyle\sum_{i}a_{i}=\sum_{i}\frac{a_{i}}{b_{i}}\cdot b_{i}\leq\max_{i}\left\{\frac{a_{i}}{b_{i}}\right\}\cdot\sum_{i}b_{i},\quad\forall a_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\,\,b_{i}>0,

(B.13)

which gives that for any fixed dual variable
0\leq\lambda\leq c\cdot r_{\max}
, we have the following inequalities,

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}-1

(B.14)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}\left(\frac{v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)\right)

(B.15)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}\cdot\max_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}\left\{\left(\frac{v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{v_{i}}\right\}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}

(B.16)

\displaystyle\qquad=\max_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}\left\{\left(\frac{v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{v_{i}}\right\},

(B.17)

where the inequality uses (
B.13
).
To further upper bound the right hand side of (
B.17
), we utilize the pessimistic property of
\bm{v}^{\mathrm{LCB}}
in Lemma
D.5
to derive that for any item
i\in S^{\star}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{v_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{1}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\cdot\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}=\frac{\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}.

(B.18)

Therefore, we can upper bound
Term (i)
via the following,

\displaystyle\text{Term (i)}\leq c\cdot r_{\max}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}.

(B.19)

Upper bounding
Term (ii).

To upper bound
Term (ii)
, we consider the following approach,

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}-1

(B.20)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}\cdot\left|\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}\left(\frac{v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)\right|

(B.21)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}\cdot\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)}\cdot\left|\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}\left(\frac{v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}\right)\cdot\big(1-\exp(-r_{i}/\lambda)\big)\right|

(B.22)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\exp(r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot\big(1-\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\big)\cdot\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}\left|\frac{v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}\right|,

(B.23)

where the first inequality just uses
x\leq|x|
, while the second inequality uses
|\sum_{i}a_{i}b_{i}|\leq\max_{i}|b_{i}|\cdot\sum_{i}|a_{i}|
.
We further upper bound the right handside of (
B.23
) as following.
On the one hand, using the fact that
\bm{v}^{\mathrm{LCB}}
is a pessimistic estimation of
\bm{v}
(Lemma
D.5
), we can derive that

\displaystyle\frac{v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}\leq\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}=\frac{v_{i}\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\right)}{\left(\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\right)},

(B.24)

and that

\displaystyle\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}-\frac{v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}\leq\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}=\frac{v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}},

(B.25)

which together gives that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}\left|\frac{v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}-\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}\max\left\{\frac{v_{i}\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\right)}{\left(\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\right)},\frac{v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}\right\}

(B.26)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}\frac{v_{i}\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\right)}{\left(\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}\right)}+\frac{v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}

(B.27)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}.

(B.28)

Here the last inequality uses the fact that
\max\{a,b\}\leq a+b
for any
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
.
On the other hand, by Lemma
D.7
, we have that for any
\lambda\in[c\cdot r_{\max},r_{\max}/\rho]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\exp(r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot\big(1-\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\big)\leq c\cdot r_{\max}\cdot\big(\exp(1/c)-1\big),

(B.29)

Consequently, combining (
B.28
) and (
B.29
), we can bound
Term (ii)
by

\displaystyle\text{Term (ii)}\leq 2c\cdot r_{\max}\cdot\big(\exp(1/c)-1\big)\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}.

(B.30)

Combining the bounds.

Now combining (
B.19
) and (
B.30
), we can derive that for any
c\in[0,1/\rho]
,

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v}^{\mathrm{LCB}})

\displaystyle\leq\max\big\{\text{Term (i)},\text{Term (ii)}\big\}

(B.31)

\displaystyle=c\cdot r_{\max}\cdot\max\big\{2,\exp(1/c)-1\big\}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}.

(B.32)

In the meanwhile, by using the same argument as in bounding
Term (i)
, i.e., (
B.19
), except that the dual variable
\lambda
is in the full range
[0,r_{\max}/\rho]
, we can obtain that

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v}^{\mathrm{LCB}})\leq\frac{r_{\max}}{\rho}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}.

(B.33)

Consequently, by combining (
B.5
), (
B.32
) and (
B.33
), we can obtain that

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(\widehat{S};\bm{v})

\displaystyle\leq R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v}^{\mathrm{LCB}})

(B.34)

\displaystyle\leq r_{\max}\cdot\min\left\{\inf_{c\in[0,1/\rho]}\Big\{c\cdot\max\big\{2,\exp(1/c)-1\big\}\Big\},\frac{1}{\rho}\right\}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i\in S_{+}^{\star}}v_{i}-v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}_{+}}v_{i}^{\mathrm{LCB}}}.

(B.35)

Now applying Lemma
D.6
, we can obtain the desired result as follows, with probability at least
1-2\delta
,

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S^{\star};\bm{v})-R_{\rho}(\widehat{S};\bm{v})

(B.36)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{\mathsf{C}(\rho)\cdot r_{\max}}{1+v(S^{\star})}\cdot\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\left(16\sqrt{\frac{v_{i}(1+v_{i})\log(3N/\delta)}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{i_{k}\in S_{k}\}\cdot(1+v(S_{k}))^{-1}}}+\frac{88(1+v_{i})\log(3N/\delta)}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{i_{k}\in S_{k}\}\cdot(1+v(S_{k}))^{-1}}\right).

(B.37)

where the function
\mathsf{C}(\rho)
is defined as
\mathsf{C}(\rho):=\min\{\inf_{c\in[0,1/\rho]}\{c\cdot\max\{2,\exp(1/c)-1\}\},\rho^{-1}\}
, and
v(S)
is defined as
v(S):=\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}

for any assortment
S\subset[N]
.
∎

B.3
Proof of Theorem
5.1

With the general results in Theorem
B.1
, we are ready to specify it to Theorem
5.1
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We prove the non-uniform revenue case and the uniform revenue case respectively as following.
Since
\mathsf{C}(\rho)=\mathcal{O}(1)
, it suffices to calculate the upper bounds for

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+v(S^{\star})}\cdot\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\sqrt{\frac{v_{i}(1+v_{i})\log(3N/\delta)}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{i_{k}\in S_{k}\}\cdot(1+v(S_{k}))^{-1}}},

(B.38)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+v(S^{\star})}\cdot\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\frac{(1+v_{i})\log(3N/\delta)}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{i_{k}\in S_{k}\}\cdot(1+v(S_{k}))^{-1}},

(B.39)

respectively, which results in different upper bounds under different setups.

Non-uniform revenue case.

For the first term in Theorem (
B.39
), we have the following upper bound,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\sum_{j\in S^{\star}}v_{j}}\cdot\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\sqrt{\frac{v_{i}(1+v_{i})\log(3N/\delta)}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bm{1}\{i\in S_{k}\}(1+\sum_{j\in S_{k}}v_{j})^{-1}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{1}{1+\sum_{j\in S^{\star}}v_{j}}\cdot\sqrt{(1+v_{\max})(1+Kv_{\max})\log(3N/\delta)}\cdot\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\sqrt{\frac{v_{i}}{n_{i}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sqrt{\frac{(1+v_{\max})(1+Kv_{\max})\log(3N/\delta)}{1+\sum_{j\in S^{\star}}v_{j}}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\frac{1}{n_{i}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(1+v_{\max})K\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(3N/\delta)}{\min_{i\in S^{\star}}n_{i}}},

where the first inequality uses that
v_{i}\leq v_{\max}
and the fact that

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{j\in S_{k}}v_{j}\leq 1+Kv_{\max},\quad\forall k\in[n],

(B.40)

the second inequality uses Cauchy-Schwartz inequality that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\sqrt{\frac{v_{i}}{n_{i}}}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}v_{i}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\frac{1}{n_{i}}}\leq\sqrt{1+\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}v_{i}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\frac{1}{n_{i}}},

(B.41)

and the last inequality uses the fact that
1+Kv_{\max}\leq K(1+v_{\max})
and the inequalities that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\frac{1}{n_{i}}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{K}{\min_{i\in S^{\star}}n_{i}}},\quad 1\leq 1+\sum_{j\in S^{\star}}v_{j}.

(B.42)

For the second term in (
B.39
), we similarly have the following upper bound,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\sum_{j\in S^{\star}}v_{j}}\cdot\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\frac{(1+v_{i})\log(3N/\delta)}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bm{1}\{i\in S_{k}\}(1+\sum_{j\in S_{k}}v_{j})^{-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1+v_{\max})^{2}K^{2}\log(3N/\delta)}{\min_{i\in S^{\star}}n_{i}}.

This finishes the proof of Theorem
5.1
for the non-uniform revenue case.

Uniform revenue case.

In the uniform revenue setting, due to Proposition
3.2
, we always have that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}v_{i}\geq\sum_{i\in S}v_{i},\quad\text{for any assortment $S$ with $|S|\leq K$}.

(B.43)

Consequently, the first term in (
B.39
) can be upper bounded as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\sum_{j\in S^{\star}}v_{j}}\cdot\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\sqrt{\frac{v_{i}(1+v_{i})\log(3N/\delta)}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bm{1}\{i\in S_{k}\}(1+\sum_{j\in S_{k}}v_{j})^{-1}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sqrt{\frac{(1+v_{\max})\log(3N/\delta)}{1+\sum_{j\in S^{\star}}v_{j}}}\cdot\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\sqrt{\frac{v_{i}}{n_{i}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sqrt{\frac{(1+v_{\max})K\log(3N/\delta)}{\min_{i\in S^{\star}}n_{i}}},

where the first inequality uses (
B.43
) and
v_{i}\leq v_{\max}
, the second inequality uses Cauchy-Schwartz inequality that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\sqrt{\frac{v_{i}}{n_{i}}}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}v_{i}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\frac{1}{n_{i}}}\leq\sqrt{1+\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}v_{i}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\frac{1}{n_{i}}},

(B.44)

together with the fact that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\frac{1}{n_{i}}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{K}{\min_{i\in S^{\star}}n_{i}}}.

(B.45)

Similarly, for the second term in (
B.39
), we have the following upper bound that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}v_{i}}\cdot\sum_{i\in S^{\star}}\frac{(1+v_{i})\log(3N/\delta)}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bm{1}\{i\in S_{k}\}(1+\sum_{j\in S_{k}}v_{j})^{-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1+v_{\max})K\log(3N/\delta)}{\min_{i\in S^{\star}}n_{i}},

as desired, finishing the proof of Theorem
5.1
for the uniform revenue case.
∎

B.4
Proof of Theorem
5.2

In this section, we prove the suboptimality lower bounds for Example
2.1
.
We first prove the lower bound for the general non-uniform revenue case, after which we prove the lower bound for the uniform revenue case.

Proof of Theorem
5.2
: non-uniform revenue case
.

We utilize the construction of the hard instances proposed by
Han et al. 2025
.
Consider that we are given the number of products
N
, the cardinality constraint
K
, the effective sample size
n_{\min}
, and the robust set size
\rho
satisfying

\displaystyle K\geq C_{0},\quad N\geq 5K,\quad n_{\min}\geq K^{2},\quad\underline{c}_{\rho}\cdot\log 2\leq\rho\leq(1-\overline{c}_{\rho})\cdot\log 2.

(B.46)

Then we can parameterize each instance of the robust assortment optimization problem by the attraction parameters of the nominal choice model and the revenues, denoted by
(\bm{v},\bm{r})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}_{+}
.
The optimal robust assortment with size constraint
K
associated with
(\bm{v},\bm{r})
is denoted by
S_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}^{\star}
.
The data generation distribution conditioning on an assortment
S
, i.e., the nominal choice probability given
S
, is denoted by
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{v}}(\cdot|S)
.

To help present the whole proof of Theorem
5.2
, we divide the proof into the following steps.

Step 1: construction of hard instance class and offline dataset.

We denote the hard instance class we are going to construct as
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{N}
.
Consider the divisions of the whole item set
[N]
in the form of

\displaystyle[N]=\mathcal{N}^{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}\cup\mathcal{N}^{0},\quad\text{with}\quad|\mathcal{N}^{\star}|=K,\,\,\mathcal{N}^{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{c}}=[4K],\,\,\mathcal{N}^{0}=[N]\setminus[4K].

(B.47)

By such a rule, the division is fully decided by the choice of the
K
-sized subset
\mathcal{N}^{\star}
of
[4K]
.
The elements of
\mathcal{V}
correspond to different ways of dividing
[N]
following such a rule.
More specifically, given any partition
(\mathcal{N}^{\star},\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}},\mathcal{N}^{0})
satisfying the rule (
B.47
), an instance
(\bm{v},\bm{r})
is defined by

\displaystyle\big(\bm{v}_{\mathcal{N}^{\star}},\bm{r}_{\mathcal{N}^{\star}}\big):=\left(\frac{1}{K}+\epsilon,r_{\max}\right),\quad\big(\bm{v}_{\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}},\bm{r}_{\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}}\big)=\left(\frac{1}{K},r_{\max}\right),\quad\big(\bm{v}_{\mathcal{N}^{0}},\bm{r}_{\mathcal{N}^{0}}\big)=(1,0),

(B.48)

where the notation
\bm{v}_{\mathcal{N}}=v
is an abbreviate for “
v_{i}=v
for any
i\in\mathcal{N}
”.
We choose the parameter
\epsilon
as

\displaystyle\epsilon:=\sqrt{\frac{C_{1}}{80n_{\min}}},

(B.49)

where
C_{1}>0
is an absolute constant specified later in Lemma
B.2
.

To specify the collection of divisions
(\mathcal{N}^{\star},\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}},\mathcal{N}^{0})
that determines
\mathcal{V}
, we invoke the following lemma.

Lemma B.2

(Packing number)
.

For a sufficiently large
K
, there exists a collection
\mathcal{F}
of
K
-sized subsets of
[4K]
so that
\Delta(S,S^{\prime})\geq\lfloor K/4\rfloor
for any
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
and that
\log\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert\geq C_{1}\cdot K
for some absolute constant
C_{1}
.

Proof of Lemma
B.2
.

Please refer to Lemma 5.1 in
Han et al. 2025
for a detailed proof.
∎

We construct the hard instance class
\mathcal{V}
induced by the packing set of
K
-sized subset of
[4K]
in Lemma
B.2
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}:=\Big\{\text{$(\bm{v},\bm{r})$ induced by $(\mathcal{N}^{\star},\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}},\mathcal{N}^{0})$ via \eqref{eq:_proof_lower_bound_0}}\,\Big|\,\text{$\mathcal{N}^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}$ with $\mathcal{F}$ given by Lemma~\ref{lem-packing-number}}\Big\}.

(B.50)

The following shows that the optimal robust assortment induced by a division
(\mathcal{N}^{\star},\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}},\mathcal{N}^{0})
is exactly
\mathcal{N}^{\star}
.

Lemma B.3

(Optimal robust assortment for class (
B.50
))
.

For any problem instance
(\bm{v},\bm{r})\in\mathcal{V}
associated with the division
(\mathcal{N}^{\star},\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}},\mathcal{N}^{0})
, its optimal robust assortment
S^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}
in the sense of Example
2.1
is
S^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}=\mathcal{N}^{\star}
.

Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

By definition, for any instance
(\bm{v},\bm{r})
with partitions given by
(\mathcal{N}^{\star},\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}},\mathcal{N}^{0})
, the robust expected revenue of any assortment
S
with
|S|\leq K
is given by

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S;\bm{v},\bm{r})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{1+|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{0}|+e^{-\frac{r_{\max}}{\lambda}}\cdot(1/K\cdot|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}|+(1/K+\epsilon)\cdot|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\star}|)}{1+|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{0}|+1/K\cdot|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}|+(1/K+\epsilon)\cdot|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\star}|)}\right)-\lambda\rho\right\}

(B.51)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(e^{-\frac{r_{\max}}{\lambda}}+\frac{(1-e^{-\frac{r_{\max}}{\lambda}})\cdot(1+|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{0}|)}{1+|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{0}|+1/K\cdot|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}|+(1/K+\epsilon)\cdot|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\star}|)}\right)-\lambda\rho\right\}

(B.52)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(e^{-\frac{r_{\max}}{\lambda}}+\frac{1-e^{-\frac{r_{\max}}{\lambda}}}{1+\frac{1}{K}\cdot\frac{|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}|}{1+|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{0}|}+\left(\frac{1}{K}+\epsilon\right)\cdot\frac{|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\star}|}{1+|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{0}|}}\right)-\lambda\rho\right\}

(B.53)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(e^{-\frac{r_{\max}}{\lambda}}+\frac{1-e^{-\frac{r_{\max}}{\lambda}}}{1+\left(\frac{1}{K}+\epsilon\right)\cdot|\mathcal{N}^{\star}|}\right)-\lambda\rho\right\},

(B.54)

where the last inequality becomes equality only when the assortment
S=\mathcal{N}^{\star}
. This proves Lemma
B.3
.
∎

Finally, we construct the observational data
\mathbb{D}=\{(S_{k},i_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n}
.
This boils down to specify the assortments
\{S_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
.
We let
n=4Kn_{\min}
and define each
S_{k}
as

\displaystyle S_{k}:=\left\{\left\lceil\frac{k}{n_{\min}}\right\rceil,4K+2,\cdots,5K\right\}.

(B.55)

Intuitively, each
S_{k}
contains
K-1
items in
\mathcal{N}^{0}
and just one item in
\mathcal{N}^{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}
.
Each item in
\mathcal{N}^{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}
appears in
n_{\mathrm{min}}
of the
n
assortments.
Therefore, for any instance
(\bm{v},\bm{r})\in\mathcal{V}
, the effective sample size
\min_{i\in S^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}}n_{i}
is exactly
n_{\min}
.
The choices
\{i_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
are independently generated obeying
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{v}}(\cdot|S_{k})
for
k\in[n]
.

Step 2: establish the minimax lower bound.

For any
(\bm{v},\bm{r})\in\mathcal{V}
and any assortment
S\subseteq[N]
satisfying
|S|\leq K
, we need to lower bound the suboptimality of
S
under instance
(\bm{v},\bm{r})
.
Actually, it suffices to consider assortments
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}=[4K]
with
|S|=K
.
This is because using a similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
D.2
, we can show that for any
S\subseteq[N]
with
|S|\leq K
, under any instance
(\bm{v},\bm{r})
,

\displaystyle R_{\rho}(S;\bm{v},\bm{r})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)+\frac{1-\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)}{1+\frac{1}{K}\cdot\frac{|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}|}{1+|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{0}|}+\left(\frac{1}{K}+\epsilon\right)\cdot\frac{|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\star}|}{1+|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{0}|}}\right)-\lambda\rho\right\}

(B.56)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)+\frac{1-\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)}{1+\frac{1}{K}\cdot(K-|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\star}|)+\left(\frac{1}{K}+\epsilon\right)\cdot|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\star}|}\right)-\lambda\rho\right\}

(B.57)

\displaystyle=R_{\rho}(\widetilde{S}(S);\bm{v},\bm{r}),\quad\text{where}\quad\widetilde{S}(S):=(S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\star})\cup\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}_{K-|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\star}|}\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}},

(B.58)

where
(\mathcal{N}^{\star},\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}},\mathcal{N}^{0})
is the division associated with the instance
(\bm{v},\bm{r})
, and
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}_{K-|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\star}|}
is any
(K-|S\cap\mathcal{N}^{\star}|)
-sized subset of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}
.
That being said, it suffices to consider all assortments
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{c}}=[4K]
with
|S|=K
.
To this end, for any such assortment, we have the following (with abbreviation
S^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}
as
S^{\star}
if no confusion),

\displaystyle\mathrm{SubOpt}_{\rho}(S;\bm{v},\bm{r})=R_{\rho}(S^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}};\bm{v},\bm{r})-R_{\rho}(S;\bm{v},\bm{r})

(B.59)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+K\epsilon)}{2+K\epsilon}\right)-\lambda\rho\right\}

(B.60)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}{2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon}\right)-\lambda\rho\right\}

(B.61)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq-\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)\cdot\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S))\cdot(1+K\epsilon)}{2+K\epsilon}\cdot\frac{2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon}{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S))\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}\right)

(B.62)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq-\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)\cdot\left(\frac{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S))\cdot(1+K\epsilon)}{2+K\epsilon}\cdot\frac{2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon}{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S))\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}-1\right)

(B.63)

\displaystyle\qquad=\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)\cdot\frac{(1-\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}^{\star}(S)))\cdot(2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S))\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2+K\epsilon}\right)

(B.64)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\frac{\epsilon}{18}\cdot\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)\cdot\frac{(1-\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)))\cdot(2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S))\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}\cdot\Delta(S^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}},S)

(B.65)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\frac{\epsilon}{18}\cdot\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)\cdot\big(1-\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S))\big)\cdot\Delta(S^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}},S),

(B.66)

where the first equality uses the dual representation of the robust expected revenue (Proposition
2.1
), the first inequality utilizes the notion of
\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)
defined as

\displaystyle\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S):=\mathop{\mathrm{argsup}}_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}{2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon}\right)-\lambda\rho\right\},

(B.67)

the second inequality uses
\log(1+x)\leq x
for
x>-1
, and the third inequality uses the fact that that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2+K\epsilon}\geq\frac{(K-|S\cap S^{\star}|)\cdot\epsilon}{(2+K\epsilon)^{2}}\geq\frac{\epsilon}{18}\cdot\underbrace{\Delta(S^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}},S)}_{\displaystyle{:=\Delta_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)}}.

(B.68)

Step 2.1: upper and lower bound the dual variable.

Now we need to upper and lower bound the dual variable
\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)
defined in (
B.67
) in order to further lower bound the right hand side of (
B.66
).
Thanks to (
B.58
), we only need consider
\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)
for
(\bm{v},\bm{r})\in\mathcal{V}
and most importantly
S\subseteq[4K]
with size
|S|=K
.

On the one hand, by Proposition
2.1
, it follows directly that

\displaystyle\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)\leq B(S,\bm{v}):=\frac{r_{\max}}{\rho}\leq\frac{r_{\max}}{\underline{c}_{\rho}\cdot\log 2},

(B.69)

where we use the condition on the robust set size
\rho
that

\displaystyle\rho\geq\underline{c}_{\rho}\cdot\log 2.

(B.70)

On the other hand, we now lower bound the dual variable as follows.
For simplicity, let’s define the dual representation function as following, given
(\bm{v},\bm{r};S)\in\mathcal{V}\times 2^{[4K]}
with
|S|=K
,

\displaystyle h_{\bm{v},\bm{r},S}(\lambda):=-\lambda\cdot\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}{2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon}\right)-\lambda\rho.

(B.71)

Noting that
h_{\bm{v},\bm{r},S}
is a smooth and concave function on
(0,\infty)
, in order to prove
\lambda^{\star}_{\bm{v},\bm{r}}(S)\geq\underline{\lambda}
for some
\underline{\lambda}>0
, it suffices to prove the following inequality,

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}h_{\bm{v},\bm{r},S}(\underline{\lambda})\geq 0.

(B.72)

To this end, we first calculate the derivative of
h_{\bm{v},\bm{r},S}
with respect to
\lambda
as following,

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}h_{\bm{v},\bm{r},S}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\rho-\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}{2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon}\right)

(B.73)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{r_{\max}}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}

(B.74)

\displaystyle=\log\left(2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon\right)-\rho-\log\Big(1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)\Big)

(B.75)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{r_{\max}}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}

(B.76)

\displaystyle\geq\overline{c}_{\rho}\cdot\log\left(2+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon\right)-\log\Big(1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)\Big)

(B.77)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{r_{\max}}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)}{1+\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\cdot(1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon)},

(B.78)

where the last inequality uses the condition on the robust set size
\rho
that

\displaystyle\rho\leq(1-\overline{c}_{\rho})\cdot\log 2.

(B.79)

Now we need to figure out the largest possible
\underline{\lambda}>0
such that
\partial h_{\bm{v},\bm{r},S}(\underline{\lambda})\geq 0
holds.
To this end, we consider the following reparametrization to simplify the notations and calculation,

\displaystyle x:=1+|S\cap S^{\star}|\cdot\epsilon\in[1,2],\quad t:=\exp(-r_{\max}/\lambda)\in[0,1],\quad u=t\cdot x\in[0,2],

(B.80)

where the upper bound on
x
originates from the choice of
\epsilon
in (
B.49
).
With (
B.80
), we can further handle the right hand side of (
B.78
) as

\displaystyle\partial h_{\bm{v},\bm{r},S}(\underline{\lambda})

\displaystyle\geq\overline{c}_{\rho}\cdot\log(1+x)-\log(1+u)+\log\left(\frac{u}{x}\right)\cdot\frac{u}{1+u}

(B.81)

\displaystyle\geq\overline{c}_{\rho}\cdot\log(1+x)-\log(1+u)+\log\left(\frac{u}{2}\right)\cdot u,

(B.82)

Now consider taking
u=(c^{\dagger}_{\rho}\wedge\overline{c}_{\rho})/2\cdot\log(1+x)\leq 2
, where
c^{\dagger}_{\rho}
is given by solving the equation that

\displaystyle c^{\dagger}_{\rho}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{c\geq 0}\left\{c\cdot\log\left(\frac{\log 2}{4}\cdot c\wedge\overline{c}_{\rho}\right)\geq-\overline{c}_{\rho}\right\}.

(B.83)

It holds that
c^{\dagger}_{\rho}>0
is an absolute constant which only dependents on the constant
\overline{c}_{\rho}
.
With such a choice of
u
, we can lower bound (
B.82
) via

\displaystyle\partial h_{\bm{v},\bm{r},S}(\underline{\lambda})
